855. GEORGE CARD was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling house of David Richards, between the hours of six and seven, the said David, and Mary, and William Sellbourn, then being in the said dwelling house, and feloniously stealing a man's hat, value 5 s. four silver tea-spoons, value 4 s. and a pair of horse pistols, value 10 s. his property.
(The witnesses examined apart.)
DAVID RICHARDS sworn.
I live in Wyche-street, the back of St. Clements.
Who had you in the house with you? - William Sellbourn, he is a lodger of mine; and Mary Richards, she is my wife; I had some other lodgers; the house was broke open between six and seven, on Monday the 11th of September, Mary Richards, myself, and William Sellbourn, were in the house; I heard a noise, as I was laying in bed, in a room opposite me; I immediately jumped out of the room; and as soon as I came out of the room, there were two men opposite to me; I immediately pursued them, and took one; one of them got off.
How high was this? - In the one pair of stairs; I slept in the one pair stairs; I pursued, and took one of them, which is the prisoner; I have the key of the room, where I found the prisoner; the lock must have been picked; I locked it myself; it was open in the morning.
How got they into the house? - I cannot tell indeed; the things were in, in the evening, and in the morning they were gone; the hat was found on the stairs; the other things were never found; they jumped down stairs when I unlocked my door, and ran out; the street door was open when they came to the bottom of the stairs.
Perhaps some of the lodgers might have gone out before that time in the morning? - I always lock that room every night, and take the key into my room; I never saw the prisoner before.
Mr. Peatt, Prisoner's Counsel. Your's is a common lodging house? - Yes.
Your lodgers come in and go out at all hours? - Yes.
Are there more keys to your house than one? - No, they are working people; they generally go out early in the morning.
Did you ever try the other keys of your house? - No one opens it that I know of.
Are they men or women that lodge in your house? - Men and women both; I saw the prisoner in the room.
Do you know the person that was with him? - No.
Were they acquainted with any of your lodgers? - I do not know.
Is the prisoner acquainted with any of your lodgers? - I do not know.
MARY RICHARDS sworn.
I am wife of David Richards; I remember being alarmed with a noise, on Monday, the 11th of September, between six and seven; my husband got out of the bed, and the moment he opened the door, to my thoughts, he fell from the top of the stairs to the bottom; I immediately got out of bed, and he brought up the prisoner.
Who lives in the room? - We keep it ourselves; it was locked the night before, and the key I brought into our bed room.
Did you examine the windows, to see if any body had got through the window? - No, Sir, there was no marks of that; I missed two tea-spoons, the one marked, the other unmarked; two pistols, a hat of my husband's; I never knew the prisoner before.
Mr. Peatt. There were a good many people come backwards and forwards to your lodgers? - Yes; no key will open that door, of any other of the doors to my knowledge.
WILLIAM SELLBOURN sworn.
I am a lodger in the two pair of stairs room; on Monday, the 11th of September, as I was sitting in my own room, between six and seven, I heard a great noise on the stairs.
Had you been out that morning? - No, I had not, nobody had been out to my knowledge; and I heard a voice, crying out murder, murder, robbers, robbers, help, help; accordingly I ran down stairs as fast as I could, and at the bottom of the stairs, I found Mr. Richards in his shirt, and the prisoner; I know nothing about the lodgers.
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
I leave it all to my counsel.
What are you? - I am a carpenter.
Prosecutor. He told me he lived in Moorfields.
GUILTY.
The prisoner called four witnesses, who gave him a very good character.
To be transported for seven years.
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. Baron EYRE.
[trialAccount t17861025-98]
856. HARDWICK RICHARDSON was indicted for feloniously assaulting Mary the wife of John Bell , on the King's highway, on the 20th of October last, and putting her in corporal fear and danger of her life, and feloniously taking from her person, and against her will, a cloth waistcoat, value 3 s. a white corded dimity ditto, value 3 s. the property of the said John.
MARY BELL sworn.
I am wife of John Bell; he lives in Crane-court, Rosemary-lane; he deals in clothes I was unlocking my own door; I lost two waistcoats; I was coming home with a parcel of waistcoats on my arm.
How many? - As many as I could conveniently take on my arm; as I was unlocking the door; it was a spring-lock, and I could not conveniently unlock it with one hand, so I put my waistcoats into my apron; then I took my two hands to undo the door; then a man came and seized me by the arm; he pulled my arm, and the waistcoats upon it; and there happened to be two waistcoats upon it; the rest were in my apron; but the second time he pulled, he got the two waistcoats away; I cried, oh, dear, who is that; he made no answer; he got half a yard from me, and dropped one of the waistcoats; I thought it was somebody in joke, as he was laughing so hearty; the neighbours were alarmed; it was early in the night; and the prisoner was stopped; when he heard me cry out, he cryed out faster than any body else.
He said nothing to you? - Nothing at all; I got my waistcoats; they were stopped, and brought back with a man, but I cannot swear this is the man; he was in a blue jacket.
JOHN FEARNE sworn.
I stopped the prisoner; I keep a shoemaker's shop the bottom of the Minories; I heard the cry of stop thief; the man came running by the door; my brother tripped him up; he fell, and I picked him up, and picked up the waistcoats too.
(The waistcoats produced, and deposed to.)
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
My Lord, I had been at work on board a ship last Friday se'night; as I had been many days before at Ratcliffe-cross; I came on shore at six in the evening, along with three or four of our people, sailors, we had three or four tankards of porter together; I quitted their company to go to Gray's Inn; I had seen them the night before; I was going through Rosemary-lane, between seven and eight, I saw a man come running out in a violent haste; I never saw the man before in my life; this woman was in pursuit of him; I was in liquor, and I ran after him; I overtook him, and had a scuffle with him; I perceived he had some property in his hand, and I snatched it out of his hand, and run after him; I was just passing this gentleman's door; the person I was taking up just passed the door as I did; I was thrown down, being suspected of being the person; I will assure you it is true; I am a sea-faring person; I never was in a Court in my life; I was in expectation of a friend coming, a Mr. Townsend; I sent for the gentleman I lodge with, in great Trinity-lane, and he was not at home.
Court to Fearne. Did you see any person running before? - No; my house is seven doors from the corner of the Minories; he was running towards the corner of the Minories.
GUILTY Of stealing.
Transported for seven years.
Tried by the second Middlesex Jury before Mr. Baron EYRE.
[trialAccount t17861025-99]
857. JOHN FERRILL and WILLIAM BASS were indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 21st of September, one cambrick handkerchief, value 1 s. 6 d. the property of a person unknown.
JOSEPH NICHOLSON sworn.
I saw a gentleman going on quietly; I watched the two prisoners, who followed him; I am sure they were in company together; I heard them converse together; then the prisoner Bass put his hand into the gentleman's pocket, and took out the handkerchief, and gave it to the prisoner Ferrill; as soon as they had done this, they both run away; I told the watchman to take them.

[trialAccount t17861025-100]
* 858. ROBERT SEARLES was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 16th day of September last, one guinea, value 1 l. 1 s. and two half-crowns, value 5 s. the property of William Thompson.
* Charles Hale served on the London Jury, the 7th day, in the room of Joseph Wells.
WILLIAM THOMPSON sworn.
I keep the Saracen's head, in Friday-street ; I lost a guinea, and half a crown, on the 16th of September last, about nine in the morning, out of my desk; I was informed either the prisoner, or his brother, robbed the desk; I called up the prisoner and his brother, and told them I suspected they had robbed the desk various times; I told them now I suspected they had robbed the desk of a guinea, and half a crown; with that they pulled out their money out of their pockets both of them; and out of the money that the prisoner had, there was a guinea, which I know to be the same, as I had marked it before; and there was half a crown, which I knew also; I had not put any mark upon that, the book-keeper said he had marked it, and he told me before, how he had marked it; I told them this money was my property, and I sent for a constable.
Mr. Garrow, Prisoner's Counsel. How long had this young man lived with you? - He was not a servant of mine; he was under my head porter.
This money was supposed to be taken out of your desk in your warehouse? - Yes.
Is it a place which is in general resorted to by people? - Yes, just to deliver parcels, and go away again.
Upon opening the desk, how many marked guineas did you find? - I did not open the desk at all.
Did not you see it opened? - No.
Do not you know what quantity of marked guineas there were in it? - Twenty-seven.
How long has it been the fashion to mark the money? - About two months.
Did you mark all that was put into that desk? - Yes.
Did not that money get into circulation? - No, never; this money was kept by itself on purpose.
Did you keep the key of the desk? -
I did not; I had known the mark before, because my clerk told me the mark the very same morning.
FELIX WADSWORTH sworn.
There is a desk in the warehouse; it is usually kept locked; I have the key.
Do you sit there to receive parcels, or when parcels are brought there, do you take them in, and lock them up? - I do.
Do you usually keep the desk locked? - Always.
Did you mark any money on leaving that desk? - Yes, thirty-seven guineas, and twenty shillings in silver, and four half crowns; all these I marked some days before, about four days, except seven guineas, seven guineas I marked over night; I had thirty guineas marked four or five days.
How much money had you in that desk marked? - That I cannot tell; I kept the unmarked in a purse by itself, and the marked in a leather purse, and the silver in a wooden dish.
I suppose the occasion of your marking the money was, that you had lost money, and wanted to detect the thieves? - On the 16th of September, between eight and nine, I was sitting on the steps, and the prisoner brought an old lanthorn, which his master bid him put into the warehouse; I gave the prisoner the large key to open the door; there is a key to the desk, another to the door.
What did you do with the key of the desk? - I had it in my pocket; he stopped about twenty minutes in the warehouse; and few a minutes after his taylor came down the street, and they went down to drink a glass, I sent for the key to go to wash myself; I took the large key from him, I immediately opened the desk and I saw the leather purse which had the thirty-seven guineas was reversed, turned upside down, after that I took and counted them, and there was only thirty-six, I told the silver, and I missed one of the half crowns; I locked the desk and went to my master, and told him; my master told me to go into the warehouse; and he came in and called up the prisoner and accused him; the prisoner said he never robbed him of a farthing; I told him he had taken a guinea and half-a-crown, and some halfpence that morning; my master desired him to take the money out of his pocket; accordingly my master found the guinea and half-a-crown upon him, knowing it to be mine, as he knew my mark.
Did you see the guinea and the half-crown that he produced out of his pocket? - Yes, the constable has it, it had my mark; I am sure it was in the desk when the young man went into the warehouse.
Are you sure you had not paid away any of this marked money? - No, I kept it; it was not circulating money, I kept it on purpose to catch the prisoner.
How could your desk be got open? - I suppose by the virtue of a false key.
Was that key searched for? - No.
Jury. Did you pay that money to nobody? - No, I never passed it.
What is the mark? - There is a horse upon the guinea, and I cut his leg with a dash; and on the half crown, opposite the king on the near side there is a dash.
(Shewn to the Jury.)
Mr. Garrow. You told my Lord, I think, that thirty-seven guineas had been marked for four days? - Yes.
Was the same mark on all the guineas? - All the same mark.
You could not tell one from the other? - No.
How long is it since you began marking guineas? - When I had about seven or eight; I kept adding to the heap, but never took any from it.
Do you mean to swear now, that in no one instance any one of these guineas was taken out and got into circulation; could it not happen by accident or any other way? - I never meant to take any out, it was a thing impossible, for nobody had a key to this desk but myself.
Was not this young man searched directly, to see if he had a key? - No, he was not.
Have you lost any of your marked guineas before? - Yes, several times.
How many had you got before you missed any? - I cannot say, then I kept putting more, every five or six days there was a guinea taken.
Somebody, however, at different times took out a marked one? - Yes, he had.
Had you lost any of your silver? - No; I used three different marks, these thirty-seven were marked alike.
What was the mark on the half-crown? - A dash on the tail side.
WILLIAM BISHOP sworn.
Are you this young man's master? - Yes.
Do you remember Mr. Thompson's evidence, and what be supposed had been done by this young man? - Perfectly well; Mr. Thompson called me up in the Coach-office, on the 16th of September, and told me that the prisoner had been taking money out of the desk; he ordered me to call him into the office; I called him up, and Mr. Thompson told him to take what money he had out of his pocket; and he laid down the money he had, which was part of it mine, and among that money was a guinea and half-a-crown, which he said belonged to Wadsworth our book-keeper; Wadsworth claimed it, and said it was his, and came out of the desk; the lock was not broke as I observed; he was not searched for a false key; his general character was a good one.
Mr. Garrow. Did not the prisoner desire to be searched? - I believe he said, you may search me if you will.
Prisoner. I am innocent; I said gentlemen search me; I leave the rest to my counsel; I am in the warehouse sometimes two or three hours.
Mr. Bishop. I gave him the lanthorn into the gateway, and I told him to go into the office and clean it; that accounts for some part of the time.
The prisoner called eleven witnesses who all gave him a very good character.
NOT GUILTY.
Tried by the London Jury before Mr. Baron EYRE.
